Hi, um… I guess what made me finally come in is this thing at work where I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings, even when I know I’m supposed to just sit there and listen. It’s kind of embarrassing because everyone else seems able to just sit through it, and I’m over there acting like I suddenly need something. The meetings can be so long, though—and actually our conference room is freezing, which doesn’t help—but yeah, mostly I’m here because I’m having a hard time staying seated when I’m expected to.

strategy meetings or while reading a client brief, I start out focused, but after a few minutes I realize I have been staring at the same slide or paragraph and have no idea what the last point was.\n- key: S003\n  name: Listening_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n  manifestation: When my manager gives me feedback or a client is explaining what they want changed, I can be looking right at them and nodding, then seconds later I have to ask them to repeat the part I just missed.\n- key: S004\n  name: Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n  manifestation: I’ll get most of a proposal or CRM update done, but then I leave off the last step, like sending the final version or logging the follow-up, even though I meant to finish it.\n- key: S005\n  name: Organization_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n  manifestation: My campaign notes end up split between emails, sticky notes, my planner, and random tabs, and I waste a lot of time figuring out what to do first instead of just starting.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly hunting for things I need for work, like my headset, client one-pagers, keys, or the notebook I was using ten minutes earlier.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: Honestly, what made me come in is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings when I know I’m supposed to stay seated, even if it’s just to refill water or pace by the printer.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: In team pitches I can hear myself going on too long about the campaign idea, and by the time I stop, I can tell other people were waiting for a chance to speak.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: I often jump in with an answer before a client or coworker has finished asking the question, and then I realize I answered the wrong thing or cut them off.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting my turn on client calls or in office discussions makes me restless, so I keep checking the clock or rehearsing what I want to say until I blurt it out too early.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: If I think of an idea while someone else is working through a deck, I’ll pop over or interrupt their conversation to add my thought, even when it probably could have waited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: I remember this starting way before high school—my elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but rushed, talked out of turn, forgot homework, and had a desk stuffed with advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when you're eating dinner?\n\nPatient: Dinner… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really paid as much attention to that. Like if I’m out with friends I might get distracted by the restaurant being loud or whatever, but that’s not really the thing that’s been bothering me.\n\nThe big one is honestly work — those long sales strategy meetings. I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated, like refilling my water or drifting over by the printer just to move around. That’s the part that feels embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Um… not sure, honestly. I mean, probably sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me outside of work. \n\nLike, if I’m at home I can just get up and do stuff, so it doesn’t register the same way. I’ll start making tea or check something in the other room, and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer because I saw one thing out of place—but I don’t know if that’s “restless” or just me being me. The work meetings are where it feels noticeable because I’m supposed to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and feel like you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I definitely get stuck thinking about work stuff sometimes, like a client email or whether I forgot to follow up with someone, and then that reminds me of three other things I was supposed to do.\n\nBut “repeating over and over” sounds more like… I don’t know, like a loop you can’t stop? I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Sometimes I just have a lot of thoughts bouncing around, and then I’m thinking about my calendar or what I need to pick up after work, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you're trying to push away certain thoughts or ideas that keep coming into your mind?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t think so… not like I have to check the stove five times or clean the counter over and over until it feels right. I mean, I’ll sometimes go back and look at something if I’m leaving the house, but I feel like everyone does that a little?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Reading a book… um, I don’t read a ton lately, honestly, unless it’s for work, and even then I’ll kind of skim. Studying isn’t really a big part of my life now.

Like, our office printer area is weirdly busy too, and sometimes I can kind of pretend I had a reason to go over there, but honestly I just needed to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Movies… I’m not totally sure that’s the best example for me. Like if I’m out with friends I might get distracted by the restaurant being loud or whatever, but that’s not really the thing that’s been bothering me.\n\nThe big one is honestly work — those long sales strategy meetings. I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated, like refilling my water or drifting over by the printer just to move around. I mean, probably sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me outside of work. \n\nLike, if I’m at home I can just get up and do stuff, so it doesn’t register the same way. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly work, I think. I mean, at home it can be annoying, but the reason I got worried is that it’s started showing up in performance reviews, and that feels… bigger.

The meetings can be so long, though—and actually our conference room is freezing, which doesn’t help—but yeah, mostly I’m here because I’m having a hard time staying seated when I’m expected to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless in other situations where you're supposed to be still, like when you're watching a movie or eating dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the main place I really notice it is those long strategy meetings at work. Like, our office printer area is weirdly busy too, and sometimes I can kind of pretend I had a reason to go over there, but honestly I just needed to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Movies… I’m not totally sure that’s the best example for me. It’s embarrassing because it’s not like I need to be over there, I just feel like I can’t keep sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when you're eating dinner?\n\nPatient: Dinner… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really paid as much attention to that. I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated, like refilling my water or drifting over by the printer just to move around. I mean, probably sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me outside of work. \n\nLike, if I’m at home I can just get up and do stuff, so it doesn’t register the same way. I’ll start making tea or check something in the other room, and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer because I saw one thing out of place—but I don’t know if that’s “restless” or just me being me. The work meetings are where it feels noticeable because I’m supposed to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and feel like you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I definitely get stuck thinking about work stuff sometimes, like a client email or whether I forgot to follow up with someone, and then that reminds me of three other things I was supposed to do.\n\nBut “repeating over and over” sounds more like… I don’t know, like a loop you can’t stop? I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Um… personal relationships? I don’t think so, not in any big way that I’ve noticed. Like, nobody’s sat me down and said, “Your pacing is ruining our friendship,” or anything.

That’s actually what made me come in—I’ll know I’m supposed to stay seated and pay attention, and I still end up getting up to refill my water or sort of pacing over by the printer like I’m doing something useful. It feels a little ridiculous because it’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive, I just… can’t seem to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness is mostly a problem at work or does it also cause you difficulties in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. I care a lot about doing good work and keeping accounts happy, so the idea that this could make someone not trust me or think I’m not on top of things—sorry, I’m kind of circling—but yeah, work is where it’s actually causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… personal relationships? I don’t think so, not in any big way that I’ve noticed. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
At home in a quiet room… I mean, sometimes, but it’s not the thing that pushed me to come here.

I’m not totally sure it’s anxiety, exactly. Sometimes I get up before I’ve even really thought about why—like my body is just done sitting there.

I guess there can be stress around it, but more from noticing that I’m doing it in a meeting and wondering if people are noticing too. Like, I’ll be walking to get water and then think, “Was that weird?” But I don’t know if that’s the reason I get up in the first place.

Sorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I feel anxious right before it happens.

The meetings can be so long, though—and actually our conference room is freezing, which doesn’t help—but yeah, mostly I’m here because I’m having a hard time staying seated when I’m expected to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless in other situations where you're supposed to be still, like when you're watching a movie or eating dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the main place I really notice it is those long strategy meetings at work. I know I’m supposed to stay seated, and I’ll even tell myself not to get up, but then I’ll end up refilling my water or kind of pacing near the printer like I’m doing something useful.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure about movies or dinner specifically—I’m probably thinking more about work because that’s where it’s been embarrassing. Like, our office printer area is weirdly busy too, and sometimes I can kind of pretend I had a reason to go over there, but honestly I just needed to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Movies… I’m not totally sure that’s the best example for me. It’s embarrassing because it’s not like I need to be over there, I just feel like I can’t keep sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when you're eating dinner?\n\nPatient: Dinner… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really paid as much attention to that. I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated, like refilling my water or drifting over by the printer just to move around. That’s the part that feels embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Um… not sure, honestly. I mean, probably sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me outside of work. \n\nLike, if I’m at home I can just get up and do stuff, so it doesn’t register the same way. I’ll start making tea or check something in the other room, and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer because I saw one thing out of place—but I don’t know if that’s “restless” or just me being me. The work meetings are where it feels noticeable because I’m supposed to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and feel like you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I definitely get stuck thinking about work stuff sometimes, like a client email or whether I forgot to follow up with someone, and then that reminds me of three other things I was supposed to do.\n\nBut “repeating over and over” sounds more like… I don’t know, like a loop you can’t stop? I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little? If the meeting is actually interesting, I can usually hang in there longer. But honestly, the thing that made me come in is that even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, I still end up getting up—like to refill my water, or I’ll kind of wander over and pace by the printer like I’m doing something useful.

The meetings can be so long, though—and actually our conference room is freezing, which doesn’t help—but yeah, mostly I’m here because I’m having a hard time staying seated when I’m expected to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless in other situations where you're supposed to be still, like when you're watching a movie or eating dinner?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the main place I really notice it is those long strategy meetings at work. I know I’m supposed to stay seated, and I’ll even tell myself not to get up, but then I’ll end up refilling my water or kind of pacing near the printer like I’m doing something useful.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure about movies or dinner specifically—I’m probably thinking more about work because that’s where it’s been embarrassing. Like, our office printer area is weirdly busy too, and sometimes I can kind of pretend I had a reason to go over there, but honestly I just needed to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when watching a movie?\n\nPatient: Movies… I’m not totally sure that’s the best example for me. I mean, if it’s a really long movie I might shift around, but what I keep thinking of is work — those long sales strategy meetings. That’s honestly what made me come in.\n\nI’ll know I’m supposed to stay seated, and I’ll still get up to refill my water or wander over by the printer, like I’m pretending I have a reason. It’s embarrassing because it’s not like I need to be over there, I just feel like I can’t keep sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when you're eating dinner?\n\nPatient: Dinner… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really paid as much attention to that. Like if I’m out with friends I might get distracted by the restaurant being loud or whatever, but that’s not really the thing that’s been bothering me.\n\nThe big one is honestly work — those long sales strategy meetings. I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated, like refilling my water or drifting over by the printer just to move around. That’s the part that feels embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Um… not sure, honestly. I mean, probably sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me outside of work. \n\nLike, if I’m at home I can just get up and do stuff, so it doesn’t register the same way. I’ll start making tea or check something in the other room, and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer because I saw one thing out of place—but I don’t know if that’s “restless” or just me being me. The work meetings are where it feels noticeable because I’m supposed to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and feel like you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I definitely get stuck thinking about work stuff sometimes, like a client email or whether I forgot to follow up with someone, and then that reminds me of three other things I was supposed to do.\n\nBut “repeating over and over” sounds more like… I don’t know, like a loop you can’t stop? I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Sometimes I just have a lot of thoughts bouncing around, and then I’m thinking about my calendar or what I need to pick up after work, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're trying to push away certain thoughts or ideas that keep coming into your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that “I have to push this thought away” kind of way. I mean, sometimes I’ll try not to think about a work thing because then I’ll remember another work thing, and then I’m like, wait, did I send that proposal? But that feels more like getting sidetracked than, like, a specific thought that keeps forcing its way in.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I’m not really sure what would count.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform certain actions over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I have to check the stove five times or clean the counter over and over until it feels right. I mean, I’ll sometimes go back and look at something if I’m leaving the house, but I feel like everyone does that a little?\n\nCleaning-wise, I’ll start wiping the kitchen and then notice the mail pile, and then I’m sorting coupons from, like, three months ago—which is embarrassing because who even uses paper coupons anymore—but that’s not because I feel forced to repeat it. It’s more like I got pulled into it. So… no, I don’t think I have that kind of repeated action thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless in other quiet or low-stimulation environments outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Quiet or low-stimulation… I notice it most in long sales strategy meetings, honestly. Like, I know I’m supposed to just stay seated and listen, but I’ll keep getting up anyway—refill my water, walk over near the printer, pretend I need to check something.\n\nThat’s actually kind of what made me come in, because it feels silly, but I keep doing it even when I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're reading a book or studying?\n\nPatient: Reading a book… um, I don’t read a ton lately, honestly, unless it’s for work, and even then I’ll kind of skim. Studying isn’t really a big part of my life now.\n\nBut the restless thing definitely happens in those long sales strategy meetings. That’s actually what made me come in—I’ll know I’m supposed to stay seated and pay attention, and I still end up getting up to refill my water or sort of pacing over by the printer like I’m doing something useful. It feels a little ridiculous because it’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive, I just… can’t seem to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness is mostly a problem at work or does it also cause you difficulties in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. I mean, at home it can be annoying, but the reason I got worried is that it’s started showing up in performance reviews, and that feels… bigger.\n\nThere was one client complaint that really got to me. I care a lot about doing good work and keeping accounts happy, so the idea that this could make someone not trust me or think I’m not on top of things—sorry, I’m kind of circling—but yeah, work is where it’s actually causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… personal relationships? That’s honestly what made me come in.\n\nI’ll know I’m supposed to stay seated, and I’ll still get up to refill my water or wander over by the printer, like I’m pretending I have a reason. It’s embarrassing because it’s not like I need to be over there, I just feel like I can’t keep sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless when you're eating dinner?\n\nPatient: Dinner… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really paid as much attention to that. Like if I’m out with friends I might get distracted by the restaurant being loud or whatever, but that’s not really the thing that’s been bothering me.\n\nThe big one is honestly work — those long sales strategy meetings. I keep getting up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated, like refilling my water or drifting over by the printer just to move around. That’s the part that feels embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Um… not sure, honestly. I mean, probably sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example jumping out at me outside of work. \n\nLike, if I’m at home I can just get up and do stuff, so it doesn’t register the same way. I’ll start making tea or check something in the other room, and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a drawer because I saw one thing out of place—but I don’t know if that’s “restless” or just me being me. The work meetings are where it feels noticeable because I’m supposed to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind and feel like you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I definitely get stuck thinking about work stuff sometimes, like a client email or whether I forgot to follow up with someone, and then that reminds me of three other things I was supposed to do.\n\nBut “repeating over and over” sounds more like… I don’t know, like a loop you can’t stop? I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Sometimes I just have a lot of thoughts bouncing around, and then I’m thinking about my calendar or what I need to pick up after work, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're trying to push away certain thoughts or ideas that keep coming into your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that “I have to push this thought away” kind of way. I mean, sometimes I’ll try not to think about a work thing because then I’ll remember another work thing, and then I’m like, wait, did I send that proposal? But that feels more like getting sidetracked than, like, a specific thought that keeps forcing its way in.\n\nSorry, I’m probably over-explaining. I guess I’m not really sure what would count.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform certain actions over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I have to check the stove five times or clean the counter over and over until it feels right. I mean, I’ll sometimes go back and look at something if I’m leaving the house, but I feel like everyone does that a little?\n\nCleaning-wise, I’ll start wiping the kitchen and then notice the mail pile, and then I’m sorting coupons from, like, three months ago—which is embarrassing because who even uses paper coupons anymore—but that’s not because I feel forced to repeat it. It’s more like I got pulled into it. So… no, I don’t think I have that kind of repeated action thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless in other quiet or low-stimulation environments outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Quiet or low-stimulation… I notice it most in long sales strategy meetings, honestly. Like, I know I’m supposed to just stay seated and listen, but I’ll keep getting up anyway—refill my water, walk over near the printer, pretend I need to check something.\n\nThat’s actually kind of what made me come in, because it feels silly, but I keep doing it even when I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're reading a book or studying?\n\nPatient: Reading a book… um, I don’t read a ton lately, honestly, unless it’s for work, and even then I’ll kind of skim. Studying isn’t really a big part of my life now.\n\nBut the restless thing definitely happens in those long sales strategy meetings. That’s actually what made me come in—I’ll know I’m supposed to stay seated and pay attention, and I still end up getting up to refill my water or sort of pacing over by the printer like I’m doing something useful. It feels a little ridiculous because it’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive, I just… can’t seem to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness is mostly a problem at work or does it also cause you difficulties in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. I mean, at home it can be annoying, but the reason I got worried is that it’s started showing up in performance reviews, and that feels… bigger.\n\nThere was one client complaint that really got to me. I care a lot about doing good work and keeping accounts happy, so the idea that this could make someone not trust me or think I’m not on top of things—sorry, I’m kind of circling—but yeah, work is where it’s actually causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… personal relationships? I don’t think so, not in any big way that I’ve noticed. Like, nobody’s sat me down and said, “Your pacing is ruining our friendship,” or anything.\n\nI mean, my friends joke that I’m the one who gets up a lot if we’re sitting somewhere too long, but that might just be… me. Sorry, that reminded me of this dinner last week where the service was really slow and I kept checking the front, but anyway—no, I wouldn’t say it’s causing actual relationship problems. Mostly it feels more noticeable at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to get up and move around when you're in a quiet room at home?\n\nPatient: At home in a quiet room… I mean, sometimes, but it’s not the thing that pushed me to come here.\n\nThe clearest example is still at work—those long sales strategy meetings where I know I’m supposed to just stay seated, and I’ll still end up getting up to refill my water or sort of pacing near the printer like I have a reason. At home, if I’m alone, I can just move around and it doesn’t feel as noticeable, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or stressed when you get up and move around during those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure it’s anxiety, exactly. Sometimes I get up before I’ve even really thought about why—like my body is just done sitting there.\n\nI guess there can be stress around it, but more from noticing that I’m doing it in a meeting and wondering if people are noticing too. Like, I’ll be walking to get water and then think, “Was that weird?” But I don’t know if that’s the reason I get up in the first place.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I feel anxious right before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less like getting up and moving around during those sales strategy meetings if they were more engaging or interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I care a lot about doing good work and keeping accounts happy, so the idea that this could make someone not trust me or think I’m not on top of things—sorry, I’m kind of circling—but yeah, work is where it’s actually causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… personal relationships? I don’t think so, not in any big way that I’ve noticed. Like, nobody’s sat me down and said, “Your pacing is ruining our friendship,” or anything.\n\nI mean, my friends joke that I’m the one who gets up a lot if we’re sitting somewhere too long, but that might just be… me. Sorry, that reminded me of this dinner last week where the service was really slow and I kept checking the front, but anyway—no, I wouldn’t say it’s causing actual relationship problems. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I feel anxious right before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less like getting up and moving around during those sales strategy meetings if they were more engaging or interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? But honestly, the thing that made me come in is that even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, I still end up getting up—like to refill my water, or I’ll kind of wander over and pace by the printer like I’m doing something useful.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m bored exactly. Sometimes it’s just… sitting there that long feels hard. Although, yeah, if someone’s presenting numbers in that monotone voice—sorry, that sounds mean—but then it definitely gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing on a single task for an extended period?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to zone out. Like, I’ll even care about the client, but my brain just… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stay seated for the entire meeting if someone was directly talking to you or asking you questions?\n\nPatient: If someone’s directly talking to me, I can usually stay put better, because it feels like I have a job in the conversation, you know? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more that I can’t sit still, honestly. I mean, maybe the attention part is mixed in there too, but what made me come in is that I keep getting up during those long sales strategy meetings even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated.

Like I’ll refill my water, or kind of drift over and pace by the printer like I’m doing something productive. Which is funny because our printer is always jammed anyway—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, I’ll notice afterward that I got up again, and it feels like I just couldn’t make myself stay in the chair.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that happens too. Not so much that I don’t understand the instructions, but I’ll get like… 90 percent of something done and then somehow not finish the last piece.

Like with a proposal, I’ll draft it, clean it up, maybe even attach the right deck—although half the time I’m also thinking about whether the client even reads the deck, which is a whole other thing—but then I’ll realize I never sent the final version. Or with CRM updates, I’ll put most of the notes in and then forget to log the follow-up step, even though I absolutely meant to.

It’s usually the last little step that gets me.

I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. So… no, I don’t think I have that kind of repeated action thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless in other quiet or low-stimulation environments outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Quiet or low-stimulation… I notice it most in long sales strategy meetings, honestly. Like, I know I’m supposed to just stay seated and listen, but I’ll keep getting up anyway—refill my water, walk over near the printer, pretend I need to check something.\n\nThat’s actually kind of what made me come in, because it feels silly, but I keep doing it even when I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're reading a book or studying?\n\nPatient: Reading a book… um, I don’t read a ton lately, honestly, unless it’s for work, and even then I’ll kind of skim. Studying isn’t really a big part of my life now.\n\nBut the restless thing definitely happens in those long sales strategy meetings. That’s actually what made me come in—I’ll know I’m supposed to stay seated and pay attention, and I still end up getting up to refill my water or sort of pacing over by the printer like I’m doing something useful. It feels a little ridiculous because it’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive, I just… can’t seem to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness is mostly a problem at work or does it also cause you difficulties in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. I mean, at home it can be annoying, but the reason I got worried is that it’s started showing up in performance reviews, and that feels… bigger.\n\nThere was one client complaint that really got to me. I care a lot about doing good work and keeping accounts happy, so the idea that this could make someone not trust me or think I’m not on top of things—sorry, I’m kind of circling—but yeah, work is where it’s actually causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… personal relationships? I don’t think so, not in any big way that I’ve noticed. At home, if I’m alone, I can just move around and it doesn’t feel as noticeable, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or stressed when you get up and move around during those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure it’s anxiety, exactly. Sometimes I get up before I’ve even really thought about why—like my body is just done sitting there.\n\nI guess there can be stress around it, but more from noticing that I’m doing it in a meeting and wondering if people are noticing too. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I feel anxious right before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less like getting up and moving around during those sales strategy meetings if they were more engaging or interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? I care a lot about doing good work and keeping accounts happy, so the idea that this could make someone not trust me or think I’m not on top of things—sorry, I’m kind of circling—but yeah, work is where it’s actually causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… personal relationships? I don’t think so, not in any big way that I’ve noticed. Like, nobody’s sat me down and said, “Your pacing is ruining our friendship,” or anything.\n\nI mean, my friends joke that I’m the one who gets up a lot if we’re sitting somewhere too long, but that might just be… me. Sorry, that reminded me of this dinner last week where the service was really slow and I kept checking the front, but anyway—no, I wouldn’t say it’s causing actual relationship problems. Mostly it feels more noticeable at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to get up and move around when you're in a quiet room at home?\n\nPatient: At home in a quiet room… I mean, sometimes, but it’s not the thing that pushed me to come here.\n\nThe clearest example is still at work—those long sales strategy meetings where I know I’m supposed to just stay seated, and I’ll still end up getting up to refill my water or sort of pacing near the printer like I have a reason. At home, if I’m alone, I can just move around and it doesn’t feel as noticeable, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious or stressed when you get up and move around during those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure it’s anxiety, exactly. Sometimes I get up before I’ve even really thought about why—like my body is just done sitting there.\n\nI guess there can be stress around it, but more from noticing that I’m doing it in a meeting and wondering if people are noticing too. Like, I’ll be walking to get water and then think, “Was that weird?” But I don’t know if that’s the reason I get up in the first place.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I feel anxious right before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less like getting up and moving around during those sales strategy meetings if they were more engaging or interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little? If the meeting is actually interesting, I can usually hang in there longer. But honestly, the thing that made me come in is that even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, I still end up getting up—like to refill my water, or I’ll kind of wander over and pace by the printer like I’m doing something useful.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m bored exactly. Sometimes it’s just… sitting there that long feels hard. Although, yeah, if someone’s presenting numbers in that monotone voice—sorry, that sounds mean—but then it definitely gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing on a single task for an extended period?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to zone out. But honestly, the thing that made me come in is that even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, I still end up getting up—like to refill my water, or I’ll kind of wander over and pace by the printer like I’m doing something useful.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m bored exactly. Although, yeah, if someone’s presenting numbers in that monotone voice—sorry, that sounds mean—but then it definitely gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty focusing on a single task for an extended period?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially if it’s something long or kind of dense, like a strategy meeting or reading a client brief.\n\nI’ll start out focused and think I’m following, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide—or the same paragraph—and I don’t actually know what the last point was. Like, I’ll even care about the client, but my brain just… wanders off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stay seated for the entire meeting if someone was directly talking to you or asking you questions?\n\nPatient: If someone’s directly talking to me, I can usually stay put better, because it feels like I have a job in the conversation, you know? It’s not dramatic, but I notice I’m doing it and think, “Why can’t I just stay seated like everyone else?”\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around during meetings because you're having trouble paying attention or because you just can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t sit still, honestly. Which is funny because our printer is always jammed anyway—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, I’ll notice afterward that I got up again, and it feels like I just couldn’t make myself stay in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty following instructions or completing tasks outside of work meetings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens too. Not so much that I don’t understand the instructions, but I’ll get like… 90 percent of something done and then somehow not finish the last piece.\n\nLike with a proposal, I’ll draft it, clean it up, maybe even attach the right deck—although half the time I’m also thinking about whether the client even reads the deck, which is a whole other thing—but then I’ll realize I never sent the final version.
